## Module 3: Data Collection and Databases

This module guides you through the essential processes of identifying, acquiring, and storing data, laying the groundwork for any successful data science project. You will explore various data sources, understand different data types and formats, and gain practical skills in working with both relational and non-relational databases. Furthermore, you will learn modern techniques for collecting data, including ethical web scraping and leveraging powerful APIs. By the end of this module, you'll be equipped to confidently gather the diverse data required for your analytical endeavors.

### Chapter 3.1 — Introduction to Data Sources and Types

#### Learning objectives
*   Distinguish between internal and external data sources and their implications for data science projects.
*   Categorize data into structured, semi-structured, and unstructured types, identifying their characteristics and common use cases.
*   Understand the structure and practical applications of common data file formats like CSV, JSON, and XML.
*   Recognize the importance of metadata and data provenance in ensuring data quality and reliability.

#### Detailed lesson content
As data scientists, our journey always begins with data, and understanding where that data comes from and what form it takes is paramount. Data can originate from countless places, broadly categorized into internal and external sources. Internal data is generated and owned by an organization, often residing within its operational systems, such as customer relationship management (CRM) systems, enterprise resource planning (ERP) platforms, sales databases, or transaction logs. This data is typically well-defined, structured, and directly relevant to the organization's core business processes. For instance, a bank's transaction history or a retail company's sales records are classic examples of internal data. The advantage of internal data is its direct relevance and often higher quality, as it's collected for specific business purposes. However, it might also be limited in scope, lacking external context.

External data, on the other hand, comes from outside the organization. This could include public datasets (like government statistics, weather data, or demographic information), social media feeds, web pages, market research reports, or data purchased from third-party providers. External data enriches internal data, providing context, trends, and competitive intelligence that an organization might not generate itself. For example, combining internal sales data with external economic indicators or social media sentiment can yield much deeper insights into consumer behavior. The challenge with external data often lies in its varied formats, potential inconsistencies, and the need for careful validation and cleaning. Regardless of its origin, all data needs to be understood in terms of its structure.

Data structure is typically classified into three main types: structured, semi-structured, and unstructured. Structured data is highly organized and conforms to a fixed schema, like a relational database table. It has clearly defined rows and columns, with each column having a specific data type (e.g., integer, string, date). Think of a spreadsheet or a SQL database table where every entry fits into a predefined category. This predictability makes structured data easy to store, query, and analyze using traditional tools and techniques. For example, a customer database with columns for `CustomerID`, `Name`, `Email`, and `PurchaseDate` is structured data. The rigidity of structured data ensures consistency but can be less flexible for rapidly evolving data needs.

Semi-structured data does not conform to a fixed schema but contains tags or markers to separate semantic elements and enforce hierarchies. It's a hybrid, more organized than unstructured data but more flexible than structured data. Examples include XML and JSON files, which use tags or key-value pairs to organize data. While they don't have a strict table format, they do have a defined structure that allows for programmatic parsing. For instance, a JSON object representing a product might have keys like `product_id`, `name`, `price`, and `features` (which itself could be a nested object or array). This flexibility makes semi-structured data ideal for web services, APIs, and document databases, allowing for easier evolution of data models without requiring schema migrations.

Unstructured data is the most prevalent and challenging type, comprising data that does not have a predefined format or organization. This includes text documents (emails, articles, reports), images, audio files, video files, and social media posts. The vast majority of data generated today is unstructured. While it contains a wealth of information, extracting insights requires advanced techniques like natural language processing (NLP) for text, computer vision for images, or speech recognition for audio. For example, customer reviews on an e-commerce site are unstructured text, but NLP can extract sentiment or identify common product issues. Analyzing unstructured data often involves transforming it into a structured or semi-structured format through feature extraction or embedding techniques.

Beyond structure, understanding common file formats is crucial for data scientists. The Comma Separated Values (CSV) format is perhaps the simplest and most ubiquitous for structured data. Each line in a CSV file represents a data record, and fields within a record are separated by commas. It's human-readable and easily imported into almost any spreadsheet program or data analysis tool. A common mistake with CSVs is assuming consistent delimiters or handling special characters within fields incorrectly, which can lead to parsing errors. Always inspect the first few lines of a CSV to confirm its structure and delimiter.

```csv
CustomerID,Name,Email,PurchaseDate
101,Alice Smith,alice@example.com,2023-01-15
102,Bob Johnson,bob@example.com,2023-02-20
103,Charlie Brown,charlie@example.com,2023-03-10
```

JSON (JavaScript Object Notation) is a lightweight, human-readable format for data interchange, especially popular for web applications and APIs. It represents data as key-value pairs and ordered lists of values (arrays), making it excellent for semi-structured data. JSON's hierarchical nature allows for complex data structures to be represented concisely.

```json
{
  "product_id": "P001",
  "name": "Laptop Pro",
  "price": 1200.00,
  "available": true,
  "features": ["16GB RAM", "512GB SSD", "Intel i7"],
  "reviews": [
    {"user": "Alice", "rating": 5, "comment": "Excellent performance!"},
    {"user": "Bob", "rating": 4, "comment": "Good value for money."}
  ]
}
```

XML (eXtensible Markup Language) is another widely used format for semi-structured data, particularly in enterprise systems and older web services. It uses a tree-like structure with tags to define elements and attributes. While more verbose than JSON, XML offers strong schema validation capabilities (using DTDs or XML Schemas), which can be beneficial for ensuring data integrity in complex systems.

```xml
<bookstore>
  <book category="cooking">
    <title lang="en">Everyday Italian</title>
    <author>Giada De Laurentiis</author>
    <year>2005</year>
    <price>30.00</price>
  </book>
  <book category="children">
    <title lang="en">Harry Potter</title>
    <author>J.K. Rowling</author>
    <year>2005</year>
    <price>29.99</price>
  </book>
</bookstore>
```

When working with any data, it's crucial to consider metadata – "data about data." Metadata describes the characteristics of a dataset, such as its source, creation date, last modification date, data types of columns, units of measurement, and any transformations applied. Good metadata is invaluable for understanding data provenance, ensuring reproducibility, and avoiding misinterpretations. Without proper metadata, even the cleanest dataset can become a black box, leading to incorrect analyses. Always strive to document your data sources and any processing steps meticulously.

#### Key concepts
*   **Internal Data**: Data generated and owned within an organization (e.g., sales records, CRM data).
*   **External Data**: Data sourced from outside an organization (e.g., public datasets, social media, market research).
*   **Structured Data**: Highly organized data that conforms to a fixed schema, typically stored in tables (e.g., relational databases, spreadsheets).
*   **Semi-structured Data**: Data with some organizational properties but without a rigid schema, often using tags or key-value pairs (e.g., JSON, XML).
*   **Unstructured Data**: Data without a predefined format or organization, requiring advanced techniques for analysis (e.g., text, images, audio, video).
*   **CSV (Comma Separated Values)**: A simple, text-based file format for tabular data where values are separated by commas.
*   **JSON (JavaScript Object Notation)**: A lightweight, human-readable data interchange format using key-value pairs and arrays.
*   **XML (eXtensible Markup Language)**: A markup language that defines a set of rules for encoding documents in a format that is both human-readable and machine-readable, using tags.
*   **Metadata**: Data about data, providing context, description, and information about the characteristics of a dataset.
*   **Data Provenance**: The origin and history of a piece of data, including where it came from and what transformations it has undergone.

#### Hands-on activity
**Activity: Exploring Different Data Formats with Python**

In this activity, you will use Python to create, read, and inspect data in CSV, JSON, and XML formats. This will solidify your understanding of their structures and how to programmatically interact with them.

1.  **Create a new Python script** named `data_formats_explorer.py`.
2.  **Generate and save data** in CSV, JSON, and XML formats using the provided templates.
3.  **Read and print the content** of each file to the console.
4.  **Parse and extract specific information** from each format.

**Code Template:**

```python
import csv
import json
import xml.etree.ElementTree as ET

# --- 1. CSV Data ---
csv_data = [
    ["ProductID", "Name", "Category", "Price"],
    ["P001", "Laptop", "Electronics", "1200.00"],
    ["P002", "Mouse", "Electronics", "25.50"],
    ["P003", "Keyboard", "Electronics", "75.00"]
]
csv_filename = "products.csv"

print(f"--- Writing to {csv_filename} ---")
with open(csv_filename, 'w', newline='') as file:
    writer = csv.writer(file)
    writer.writerows(csv_data)
print(f"Data written to {csv_filename}")

print(f"\n--- Reading from {csv_filename} ---")
with open(csv_filename, 'r') as file:
    reader = csv.reader(file)
    for row in reader:
        print(row)

print("\n--- Extracting specific CSV data (e.g., product names) ---")
with open(csv_filename, 'r') as file:
    reader = csv.DictReader(file) # Use DictReader for easier column access
    for row in reader:
        print(f"Product Name: {row['Name']}, Price: {row['Price']}")

# --- 2. JSON Data ---
json_data = {
    "store": {
        "name": "Tech Gadgets Inc.",
        "location": "Online",
        "products": [
            {"id": "P001", "name": "Laptop", "price": 1200.00, "in_stock": True},
            {"id": "P002", "name": "Mouse", "price": 25.50, "in_stock": False},
            {"id": "P003", "name": "Keyboard", "price": 75.00, "in_stock": True}
        ],
        "last_updated": "2023-10-26"
    }
}
json_filename = "store_data.json"

print(f"\n--- Writing to {json_filename} ---")
with open(json_filename, 'w') as file:
    json.dump(json_data, file, indent=4) # indent for pretty printing
print(f"Data written to {json_filename}")

print(f"\n--- Reading from {json_filename} ---")
with open(json_filename, 'r') as file:
    loaded_json_data = json.load(file)
    print(json.dumps(loaded_json_data, indent=4)) # print loaded data prettily

print("\n--- Extracting specific JSON data (e.g., product names and stock status) ---")
for product in loaded_json_data['store']['products']:
    print(f"Product: {product['name']}, In Stock: {product['in_stock']}")

# --- 3. XML Data ---
# Manually construct XML for demonstration
xml_root = ET.Element("inventory")
item1 = ET.SubElement(xml_root, "item", id="I001")
ET.SubElement(item1, "name").text = "Monitor"
ET.SubElement(item1, "quantity").text = "50"
ET.SubElement(item1, "unit_price").text = "300.00"

item2 = ET.SubElement(xml_root, "item", id="I002")
ET.SubElement(item2, "name").text = "Webcam"
ET.SubElement(item2, "quantity").text = "120"
ET.SubElement(item2, "unit_price").text = "70.00"

xml_tree = ET.ElementTree(xml_root)
xml_filename = "inventory.xml"

print(f"\n--- Writing to {xml_filename} ---")
xml_tree.write(xml_filename, encoding="utf-8", xml_declaration=True)
print(f"Data written to {xml_filename}")

print(f"\n--- Reading from {xml_filename} ---")
with open(xml_filename, 'r', encoding="utf-8") as file:
    print(file.read())

print("\n--- Extracting specific XML data (e.g., item names and quantities) ---")
parsed_xml_tree = ET.parse(xml_filename)
root = parsed_xml_tree.getroot()
for item in root.findall('item'):
    name = item.find('name').text
    quantity = item.find('quantity').text
    print(f"Item: {name}, Quantity: {quantity}")

```

**Instructions:**
1.  Save the code above as `data_formats_explorer.py`.
2.  Run the script from your terminal: `python data_formats_explorer.py`.
3.  Observe the output, noting how each format is structured and how Python handles reading and parsing them.
4.  Experiment by modifying the data within the Python script, saving it, and re-running to see the changes.

#### Assessment idea
1.  **Question:** A data scientist is working with a dataset that contains customer reviews submitted as free-form text, alongside a spreadsheet of product sales figures. How would you classify these two types of data, and what are the primary challenges associated with analyzing the customer reviews?
    *   **Correct Answer & Explanation:** The product sales figures in a spreadsheet are **structured data** because they conform to a fixed schema with clearly defined columns (e.g., ProductID, SalesAmount, Date). The customer reviews are **unstructured data** as they are free-form text without a predefined format. The primary challenges with analyzing customer reviews include:
        *   **Lack of direct queryability:** You cannot easily query specific values like you would in a database.
        *   **Ambiguity and nuance:** Human language is complex, with sarcasm, idioms, and context-dependent meanings that are hard for machines to interpret.
        *   **Volume and variety:** Handling large volumes of diverse text data requires scalable processing.
        *   **Need for specialized tools:** Analyzing unstructured text requires techniques from Natural Language Processing (NLP), such as sentiment analysis, topic modeling, and entity recognition, which are more complex than standard statistical methods for structured data.

2.  **Question:** You receive a file named `config.json` from a web service. What is the most likely structure of the data within this file, and what Python module would you use to efficiently read and parse its contents into a Python dictionary or list?
    *   **Correct Answer & Explanation:** A `.json` file most likely contains **semi-structured data** represented as key-value pairs and arrays, following the JavaScript Object Notation (JSON) format. To efficiently read and parse its contents into a Python dictionary or list, you would use Python's built-in `json` module. Specifically, you would use `json.load(file_object)` to read from a file or `json.loads(string_data)` to parse a JSON string. This module handles the conversion between JSON syntax and native Python data structures (dictionaries and lists).

### Chapter 3.2 — Relational Databases and SQL Fundamentals

#### Learning objectives
*   Explain the fundamental concepts of relational databases, including tables, rows, columns, primary keys, and foreign keys.
*   Write basic SQL queries to retrieve, filter, and sort data from single tables using `SELECT`, `FROM`, `WHERE`, and `ORDER BY`.
*   Perform aggregate functions and group data using `GROUP BY` and `HAVING` clauses.
*   Combine data from multiple tables using various `JOIN` operations (INNER, LEFT, RIGHT).
*   Understand the importance of data integrity and relationships in a relational database context.

#### Detailed lesson content
Relational databases have been the backbone of data storage for decades, and for good reason. They provide a robust, structured, and consistent way to manage vast amounts of data. At their core, relational databases organize data into **tables**, which are often referred to as relations. Each table represents a specific entity, like `Customers`, `Products`, or `Orders`. Within each table, data is arranged into **rows** (also known as records or tuples) and **columns** (also known as fields or attributes). Each row represents a single instance of the entity (e.g., one specific customer), and each column represents a particular attribute of that entity (e.g., customer's name, email, or address).

A critical concept in relational databases is the **primary key**. This is a column (or a set of columns) that uniquely identifies each row in a table. For example, in a `Customers` table, `CustomerID` would likely be the primary key, ensuring that no two customers have the same ID. Primary keys enforce entity integrity, preventing duplicate records and ensuring that each record can be unambiguously referenced. Related to this is the **foreign key**, which is a column (or set of columns) in one table that refers to the primary key in another table. Foreign keys establish relationships between tables, enforcing referential integrity. For instance, an `Orders` table might have a `CustomerID` column that acts as a foreign key, linking each order back to a specific customer in the `Customers` table. This relationship ensures that an order can only be placed by an existing customer.

The standard language for interacting with relational databases is **SQL (Structured Query Language)**. SQL is incredibly powerful and versatile, allowing data scientists to retrieve, insert, update, and delete data, as well as manage database schemas. For data retrieval, the `SELECT` statement is your primary tool. It specifies which columns you want to retrieve. The `FROM` clause indicates which table you're querying.

Let's imagine we have a simple `Employees` table:

```sql
-- Employees Table
-- EmployeeID (Primary Key) | FirstName | LastName | Department | Salary
-----------------------------------------------------------------------
-- 101                      | Alice     | Smith    | Sales      | 60000
-- 102                      | Bob       | Johnson  | IT         | 75000
-- 103                      | Charlie   | Brown    | Sales      | 62000
-- 104                      | Diana     | Prince   | HR         | 55000
```

To get all employee names and their departments:
```sql
SELECT FirstName, LastName, Department
FROM Employees;
```

To retrieve all columns, you can use the asterisk (`*`):
```sql
SELECT *
FROM Employees;
```

The `WHERE` clause is used to filter rows based on specified conditions. You can use comparison operators (`=`, `!=`, `<`, `>`, `<=`, `>=`), logical operators (`AND`, `OR`, `NOT`), and special operators (`LIKE`, `IN`, `BETWEEN`, `IS NULL`).

To find all employees in the 'Sales' department:
```sql
SELECT FirstName, LastName
FROM Employees
WHERE Department = 'Sales';
```

To find employees with a salary greater than 70000:
```sql
SELECT FirstName, LastName, Salary
FROM Employees
WHERE Salary > 70000;
```

Combining conditions:
```sql
SELECT FirstName, LastName, Department, Salary
FROM Employees
WHERE Department = 'Sales' AND Salary > 60000;
```

The `ORDER BY` clause sorts the result set based on one or more columns, either in ascending (`ASC`, default) or descending (`DESC`) order.

To get employees sorted by salary in descending order:
```sql
SELECT FirstName, LastName, Salary
FROM Employees
ORDER BY Salary DESC;
```

To sort by department then by last name:
```sql
SELECT FirstName, LastName, Department
FROM Employees
ORDER BY Department ASC, LastName ASC;
```

**Aggregate functions** allow you to perform calculations on a set of rows and return a single summary value. Common aggregate functions include `COUNT()`, `SUM()`, `AVG()`, `MIN()`, and `MAX()`.

To find the total number of employees:
```sql
SELECT COUNT(*)
FROM Employees;
```

To find the average salary:
```sql
SELECT AVG(Salary)
FROM Employees;
```

The `GROUP BY` clause is used with aggregate functions to group rows that have the same values in specified columns into summary rows. This is incredibly useful for analyzing data by categories.

To find the average salary per department:
```sql
SELECT Department, AVG(Salary) AS AverageSalary
FROM Employees
GROUP BY Department;
```

The `HAVING` clause is similar to `WHERE` but is used to filter the results of `GROUP BY` clauses, applying conditions to the aggregate values.

To find departments where the average salary is greater than 65000:
```sql
SELECT Department, AVG(Salary) AS AverageSalary
FROM Employees
GROUP BY Department
HAVING AVG(Salary) > 65000;
```

One of the most powerful features of relational databases is the ability to combine data from multiple tables using **`JOIN`** operations. This is where foreign keys become crucial. Let's introduce another table, `Departments`:

```sql
-- Departments Table
-- DepartmentID (Primary Key) | DepartmentName | Location
---------------------------------------------------------
-- D01                        | Sales          | New York
-- D02                        | IT             | San Francisco
-- D03                        | HR             | Chicago
```

And let's assume our `Employees` table now has `DepartmentID` as a foreign key:

```sql
-- Employees Table
-- EmployeeID | FirstName | LastName | DepartmentID | Salary
-------------------------------------------------------------
-- 101        | Alice     | Smith    | D01          | 60000
-- 102        | Bob       | Johnson  | D02          | 75000
-- 103        | Charlie   | Brown    | D01          | 62000
-- 104        | Diana     | Prince   | D03          | 55000
-- 105        | Eve       | Adams    | D04          | 80000 -- D04 not in Departments table
```

An **`INNER JOIN`** returns only the rows where there is a match in *both* tables. If an employee's `DepartmentID` doesn't exist in the `Departments` table, that employee won't appear in the result.

```sql
SELECT E.FirstName, E.LastName, D.DepartmentName, D.Location
FROM Employees AS E
INNER JOIN Departments AS D
ON E.DepartmentID = D.DepartmentID;
```
*(Note: Employee Eve Adams (ID 105) would not be included in this result because 'D04' does not exist in the Departments table.)*

A **`LEFT JOIN`** (or `LEFT OUTER JOIN`) returns all rows from the left table (the first table in the `FROM` clause) and the matching rows from the right table. If there's no match in the right table, `NULL` values will appear for the right table's columns.

```sql
SELECT E.FirstName, E.LastName, D.DepartmentName, D.Location
FROM Employees AS E
LEFT JOIN Departments AS D
ON E.DepartmentID = D.DepartmentID;
```
*(Note: Employee Eve Adams (ID 105) would be included, and her `DepartmentName` and `Location` would be `NULL`.)*

A **`RIGHT JOIN`** (or `RIGHT OUTER JOIN`) is the opposite: it returns all rows from the right table and the matching rows from the left table. If there's no match in the left table, `NULL` values appear for the left table's columns.

```sql
SELECT E.FirstName, E.LastName, D.DepartmentName, D.Location
FROM Employees AS E
RIGHT JOIN Departments AS D
ON E.DepartmentID = D.DepartmentID;
```
*(Note: If there was a department in `Departments` without any employees, that department would still appear, with `NULL` for employee details.)*

**Common Mistakes & Safety Notes:**
*   **Forgetting the `WHERE` clause:** When performing `UPDATE` or `DELETE` operations, forgetting a `WHERE` clause can lead to modifying or deleting *all* records in a table, which is usually catastrophic. Always double-check your `WHERE` clauses.
*   **Incorrect `JOIN` type:** Choosing the wrong `JOIN` (e.g., `INNER` instead of `LEFT`) can lead to silently dropping important data or including unwanted `NULL`s. Understand what each `JOIN` type does.
*   **SQL Injection:** When building queries dynamically with user input, failing to sanitize input can expose your database to SQL injection attacks. Always use parameterized queries or prepared statements to prevent this.
*   **Performance:** `SELECT *` can be inefficient for large tables as it retrieves all columns, even if not needed. Specify only the columns you require. Complex `JOIN`s or `GROUP BY` operations on very large datasets can also be slow; consider indexing relevant columns.

Mastering SQL is a fundamental skill for any data scientist, enabling direct interaction with the vast majority of organizational data. Practice is key to becoming proficient.

#### Key concepts
*   **Table (Relation)**: A collection of related data organized in rows and columns, representing an entity.
*   **Row (Record/Tuple)**: A single entry or instance within a table, containing data for each column.
*   **Column (Field/Attribute)**: A specific category of data within a table, defining the type of information stored.
*   **Primary Key**: A column or set of columns that uniquely identifies each row in a table.
*   **Foreign Key**: A column or set of columns in one table that refers to the primary key of another table, establishing a relationship.
*   **SQL (Structured Query Language)**: The standard language for managing and querying relational databases.
*   **`SELECT`**: SQL command to specify which columns to retrieve.
*   **`FROM`**: SQL clause to specify the table(s) from which to retrieve data.
*   **`WHERE`**: SQL clause to filter rows based on specified conditions.
*   **`ORDER BY`**: SQL clause to sort the result set.
*   **Aggregate Functions**: Functions like `COUNT()`, `SUM()`, `AVG()`, `MIN()`, `MAX()` that perform calculations on a set of rows.
*   **`GROUP BY`**: SQL clause to group rows that have the same values in specified columns into summary rows.
*   **`HAVING`**: SQL clause used to filter the results of `GROUP BY` based on aggregate conditions.
*   **`JOIN`**: SQL operation to combine rows from two or more tables based on a related column between them.
*   **`INNER JOIN`**: Returns only rows where there is a match in both tables.
*   **`LEFT JOIN` (or `LEFT OUTER JOIN`)**: Returns all rows from the left table and the matching rows from the right table (NULLs if no match).
*   **`RIGHT JOIN` (or `RIGHT OUTER JOIN`)**: Returns all rows from the right table and the matching rows from the left table (NULLs if no match).

#### Hands-on activity
**Activity: Querying a Sample Database with SQL**

You will practice writing SQL queries against a simulated e-commerce database. Imagine you have two tables: `Customers` and `Orders`.

`Customers` table:

`Orders` table:

*   `order_date` (DATE)
*   `total_amount` (REAL)
*   `status` (TEXT)

# Create Customers table

CREATE TABLE Customers (
    customer_id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
    first_name TEXT NOT NULL,
    last_name TEXT NOT NULL,
    email TEXT UNIQUE,
    registration_date DATE
);
''')

# Insert sample customers
cursor.execute("INSERT INTO Customers VALUES (1, 'Alice', 'Smith', 'alice@example.com', '2023-01-10');")

# Create Orders table

CREATE TABLE Orders (
    order_id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
    customer_id INTEGER NOT NULL,

    FOREIGN KEY (customer_id) REFERENCES Customers(customer_id)

print("Database and tables created with sample data.")

#### Assessment idea
1.  **Question:** Consider two tables: `Products` (with columns `ProductID` (PK), `ProductName`, `CategoryID`) and `Categories` (with columns `CategoryID` (PK), `CategoryName`). You want to retrieve a list of all product names along with their corresponding category names. Write an SQL query to achieve this.
    *   **Correct Answer & Explanation:**
        ```sql
        SELECT P.ProductName, C.CategoryName
        FROM Products AS P
        INNER JOIN Categories AS C
        ON P.CategoryID = C.CategoryID;
        ```
        **Explanation:** We use an `INNER JOIN` because we only want to see products that have a matching category and categories that have matching products. The `ON` clause specifies the join condition, which is the equality of `CategoryID` in both tables. Aliases `P` and `C` are used for brevity and clarity.

2.  **Question:** You have a table named `Sales` with columns `SaleID`, `ProductID`, `SaleDate`, and `Amount`. You need to find the total sales amount for each `ProductID` where the total sales for that product exceed $1000. Write an SQL query to accomplish this.
    *   **Correct Answer & Explanation:**
        ```sql
        SELECT ProductID, SUM(Amount) AS TotalSalesAmount
        FROM Sales
        GROUP BY ProductID
        HAVING SUM(Amount) > 1000;
        ```
        **Explanation:** First, we use `GROUP BY ProductID` to group all sales records for the same product. Then, `SUM(Amount)` calculates the total sales for each of these groups. Finally, the `HAVING` clause filters these grouped results, keeping only those products where their `TotalSalesAmount` (the aggregate sum) is greater than 1000. It's crucial to use `HAVING` for filtering aggregate results, not `WHERE`.

### Chapter 3.3 — Working with Non-Relational (NoSQL) Databases

#### Learning objectives
*   Understand the motivations and use cases for adopting NoSQL databases over traditional relational databases.
*   Identify and differentiate between the main categories of NoSQL databases: document, key-value, column-family, and graph.
*   Perform basic CRUD (Create, Read, Update, Delete) operations on a document-oriented NoSQL database like MongoDB.
*   Recognize the trade-offs between consistency, availability, and partition tolerance (CAP theorem) in NoSQL systems.
*   Determine when a NoSQL database might be a more suitable choice for a data science project compared to a relational database.

#### Detailed lesson content
While relational databases excel at managing structured, interconnected data, the modern data landscape often presents challenges that push beyond their traditional strengths. The explosion of big data, the need for extreme scalability, the prevalence of unstructured and semi-structured data, and the demand for agile development with flexible schemas have led to the rise of **NoSQL (Not only SQL)** databases. NoSQL databases offer alternative data storage and retrieval mechanisms that are often optimized for specific data models and access patterns, providing greater flexibility, scalability, and performance for certain workloads.

The primary motivations for choosing NoSQL include:
1.  **Scalability**: Many NoSQL databases are designed to scale horizontally, meaning they can distribute data across many servers (a cluster) to handle increasing data volumes and traffic, rather than relying on a single, more powerful server (vertical scaling). This is crucial for big data applications.
2.  **Flexibility**: NoSQL databases often have schema-less or flexible schemas, allowing developers to store data without a predefined structure. This is ideal for rapidly evolving applications or when dealing with diverse, semi-structured, or unstructured data, as it eliminates the need for costly schema migrations.
3.  **Performance**: By optimizing for specific data models (e.g., key-value lookups, document retrieval), NoSQL databases can offer superior performance for certain operations compared to general-purpose relational databases.
4.  **Variety of Data Models**: Instead of the single tabular model of relational databases, NoSQL offers a diverse set of data models tailored to different needs.

There are four main categories of NoSQL databases, each with distinct characteristics and use cases:

1.  **Document Databases**: These store data in flexible, semi-structured documents, typically in JSON, BSON (Binary JSON), or XML format. Each document is a self-contained unit, and documents within a collection don't necessarily need to have the same structure. This model is excellent for content management systems, e-commerce product catalogs, user profiles, and mobile applications. **MongoDB** and CouchDB are popular examples.
2.  **Key-Value Stores**: The simplest NoSQL model, storing data as a collection of key-value pairs. Keys are unique identifiers, and values can be anything from a simple string to a complex object. They offer extremely fast read/write operations for individual items. Use cases include caching, session management, and real-time data. Redis and Amazon DynamoDB (which also supports document model) are prominent examples.
3.  **Column-Family Stores**: These databases store data in tables, but unlike relational databases, they organize data by columns rather than rows. Each row can have different columns, and columns are grouped into "column families." This model is highly optimized for analytical queries over large datasets and time-series data. Apache Cassandra and HBase are well-known examples.
4.  **Graph Databases**: Designed to store and navigate relationships between data entities (nodes) through connections (edges). They are ideal for social networks, recommendation engines, fraud detection, and knowledge graphs where relationships are as important as the data itself. Neo4j is a leading graph database.

Let's focus on **MongoDB**, a popular document database, to illustrate basic NoSQL operations. MongoDB stores data in **collections** (analogous to tables in relational databases) and **documents** (analogous to rows, but with flexible schema). Each document is a BSON object.

To interact with MongoDB, you typically use the MongoDB Shell or a driver in your preferred programming language (like PyMongo for Python).

**Basic CRUD Operations in MongoDB (using MongoDB Shell syntax):**

**C - Create (Insert):**
To insert a single document into a collection called `products`:
```javascript
db.products.insertOne({
  name: "Wireless Headphones",
  brand: "AudioTech",
  price: 199.99,
  features: ["Noise Cancelling", "Bluetooth 5.0"],
  stock: 50
});
```
To insert multiple documents:
```javascript
db.products.insertMany([
  {
    name: "Smartwatch",
    brand: "WearableCo",
    price: 249.00,
    features: ["Heart Rate Monitor", "GPS"],
    stock: 120
  },
  {
    name: "Portable Speaker",
    brand: "SoundBlast",
    price: 79.99,
    features: ["Waterproof", "Long Battery Life"]
  }
]);
```

**R - Read (Find):**
To find all documents in the `products` collection:
```javascript
db.products.find({});
```
To find documents that match a specific condition (e.g., products with a price less than 100):
```javascript
db.products.find({ price: { $lt: 100 } }); // $lt means 'less than'
```
To find products by brand:
```javascript
db.products.find({ brand: "AudioTech" });
```
To find products with a specific feature (using an array field):
```javascript
db.products.find({ features: "GPS" });
```
To find documents and project (select) specific fields:
```javascript
db.products.find({ brand: "WearableCo" }, { name: 1, price: 1, _id: 0 }); // 1 to include, 0 to exclude
```

**U - Update:**
To update a single document (e.g., increase stock for "Wireless Headphones"):
```javascript
db.products.updateOne(
  { name: "Wireless Headphones" },
  { $set: { stock: 60, last_updated: new Date() } }
);
```
To update multiple documents (e.g., add a new feature to all "AudioTech" products):
```javascript
db.products.updateMany(
  { brand: "AudioTech" },
  { $push: { features: "Voice Assistant" } } // $push adds an element to an array
);
```

**D - Delete:**
To delete a single document (e.g., the "Portable Speaker"):
```javascript
db.products.deleteOne({ name: "Portable Speaker" });
```
To delete multiple documents (e.g., all products from "WearableCo"):
```javascript
db.products.deleteMany({ brand: "WearableCo" });
```

**Common Mistakes & Safety Notes:**
*   **Schema-less doesn't mean no schema:** While NoSQL databases are flexible, it's still good practice to have an implicit schema or consistent document structure within your application logic to ensure data integrity and ease of querying.
*   **Over-normalization vs. De-normalization:** Relational databases encourage normalization (reducing data redundancy). NoSQL often benefits from de-normalization (duplicating data) to optimize for read performance and reduce complex joins, which are not native to many NoSQL types. Understanding when to de-normalize is key.
*   **Choosing the wrong NoSQL type:** Don't just pick MongoDB because it's popular. Understand your data access patterns, scalability needs, and data model before committing to a specific NoSQL database. Graph databases for relationships, key-value for simple lookups, document for flexible hierarchical data.
*   **CAP Theorem:** This fundamental theorem states that a distributed system (like most NoSQL databases) can only guarantee two of three properties: Consistency, Availability, and Partition Tolerance.
    *   **Consistency:** All clients see the same data at the same time.
    *   **Availability:** Every request receives a response, without guarantee that it contains the most recent version of the information.
    *   **Partition Tolerance:** The system continues to operate despite arbitrary message loss or failure of parts of the system.
    Most NoSQL databases prioritize Availability and Partition Tolerance over strong Consistency (eventual consistency), which is a trade-off to consider for your application's requirements. Relational databases typically prioritize Consistency.

As a data scientist, you'll encounter NoSQL databases frequently, especially when working with large-scale data platforms, real-time analytics, or cloud-native applications (like those on IBM Cloud, which offers services like Cloudant, a document database). Knowing how to interact with them and understanding their strengths and weaknesses is a valuable skill.

#### Key concepts
*   **NoSQL (Not only SQL)**: A category of databases that provide alternative data storage and retrieval mechanisms to relational databases, optimized for scalability, flexibility, and specific data models.
*   **Document Database**: Stores data in flexible, semi-structured documents (e.g., JSON, BSON). Examples: MongoDB, CouchDB.
*   **Key-Value Store**: Stores data as simple key-value pairs for fast lookups. Examples: Redis, DynamoDB.
*   **Column-Family Store**: Organizes data by columns, optimized for analytical queries over large datasets. Examples: Apache Cassandra, HBase.
*   **Graph Database**: Stores data as nodes and edges, ideal for representing and traversing relationships. Example: Neo4j.
*   **Collection**: In document databases, a grouping of related documents (similar to a table).
*   **Document**: A single record in a document database, typically a JSON-like object with a flexible schema.
*   **CRUD Operations**: Acronym for Create, Read, Update, Delete – the four basic functions of persistent storage.
*   **Horizontal Scaling**: Distributing data and processing across multiple servers to handle increased load.
*   **Flexible Schema**: The ability to store data without a predefined, rigid structure, allowing documents in a collection to have different fields.
*   **CAP Theorem**: A fundamental principle in distributed systems stating that a system can only guarantee two of the three properties: Consistency, Availability, and Partition Tolerance.
*   **Eventual Consistency**: A consistency model where updates propagate through the system over time, eventually reaching all replicas, but not necessarily immediately.

#### Hands-on activity
**Activity: Basic MongoDB Operations with PyMongo**

In this activity, you will use Python with the `pymongo` library to connect to a local MongoDB instance (or a free cloud instance like MongoDB Atlas) and perform basic CRUD operations on a sample `sensor_data` collection.

**Prerequisites:**
1.  **MongoDB Installation:** Ensure you have MongoDB installed and running locally, or have access to a MongoDB Atlas cluster (free tier is sufficient).
2.  **PyMongo Installation:** Install the Python driver: `pip install pymongo`

**Code Template:**

```python
from pymongo import MongoClient
from datetime import datetime
import pprint # For pretty printing documents

# --- Configuration ---
# Replace with your MongoDB connection string.
# For local MongoDB: "mongodb://localhost:27017/"
# For MongoDB Atlas: "mongodb+srv://<username>:<password>@<cluster-url>/<dbname>?retryWrites=true&w=majority"
MONGO_URI = "mongodb://localhost:27017/" # Adjust if your MongoDB is elsewhere

DB_NAME = "iot_analytics"
COLLECTION_NAME = "sensor_data"

def connect_to_mongodb():
    """Establishes connection to MongoDB."""
    try:
        client = MongoClient(MONGO_URI)
        db = client[DB_NAME]
        print(f"Connected to MongoDB database: '{DB_NAME}'")
        return db, client
    except Exception as e:
        print(f"Error connecting to MongoDB: {e}")
        return None, None

def main():
    db, client = connect_to_mongodb()
    if not db:
        return

    collection = db[COLLECTION_NAME]

    # --- C - Create (Insert) ---
    print("\n--- Inserting documents ---")
    sensor_reading1 = {
        "sensor_id": "temp_001",
        "timestamp": datetime.now(),
        "location": "Server Room A",
        "temperature_c": 25.3,
        "humidity_percent": 45.1
    }
    result1 = collection.insert_one(sensor_reading1)
    print(f"Inserted document with _id: {result1.inserted_id}")

    sensor_reading2 = {
        "sensor_id": "hum_002",
        "timestamp": datetime.now(),
        "location": "Warehouse B",
        "humidity_percent": 68.5,
        "pressure_hpa": 1012.3
    }
    sensor_reading3 = {
        "sensor_id": "temp_001", # Same sensor, new reading
        "timestamp": datetime.now(),
        "location": "Server Room A",
        "temperature_c": 26.1,
        "humidity_percent": 46.0
    }
    result2 = collection.insert_many([sensor_reading2, sensor_reading3])
    print(f"Inserted multiple documents with _ids: {result2.inserted_ids}")

    # --- R - Read (Find) ---
    print("\n--- Finding all documents ---")
    for doc in collection.find():
        pprint.pprint(doc)

    print("\n--- Finding documents from 'Server Room A' ---")
    for doc in collection.find({"location": "Server Room A"}):
        pprint.pprint(doc)

    print("\n--- Finding documents with temperature > 25.5 ---")
    for doc in collection.find({"temperature_c": {"$gt": 25.5}}):
        pprint.pprint(doc)

    # --- U - Update ---
    print("\n--- Updating a document ---")
    update_result = collection.update_one(
        {"sensor_id": "hum_002"},
        {"$set": {"location": "Main Warehouse B", "last_calibrated": datetime.now()}}
    )
    print(f"Matched {update_result.matched_count} document(s), modified {update_result.modified_count} document(s).")
    print("\n--- Document after update: ---")
    pprint.pprint(collection.find_one({"sensor_id": "hum_002"}))

    # --- D - Delete ---
    print("\n--- Deleting documents ---")
    delete_result = collection.delete_many({"location": "Warehouse B"}) # Note: we updated hum_002's location
    print(f"Deleted {delete_result.deleted_count} document(s) from original 'Warehouse B'.")

    # Clean up: delete all documents from the collection for a fresh run next time
    # print("\n--- Cleaning up: Deleting all remaining documents ---")
    # collection.delete_many({})
    # print(f"Deleted all {collection.count_documents({})} documents from '{COLLECTION_NAME}'.")

    client.close()
    print("MongoDB connection closed.")

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()
```

**Instructions:**
1.  **Set up MongoDB:** Start your local MongoDB server (e.g., `mongod` in your terminal) or ensure your MongoDB Atlas cluster is accessible.
2.  **Update `MONGO_URI`:** Modify the `MONGO_URI` variable in the Python script to match your MongoDB connection string.
3.  **Run the script:** Execute `python your_script_name.py` from your terminal.
4.  **Observe the output:** See how documents are inserted, retrieved, updated, and deleted.
5.  **Experiment:**
    *   Add more complex queries using `$and`, `$or`, `$in` operators.
    *   Try updating multiple documents with `update_many`.
    *   Add a `delete_one` operation.
    *   Inspect your MongoDB database directly (e.g., using MongoDB Compass or the `mongo` shell) to see the changes.

#### Assessment idea
1.  **Question:** A social media company needs to store user profiles. Each profile can have a varying number of fields (e.g., some users provide a bio, others don't; some have many friends, others few). Additionally, the company anticipates rapid feature development, which might introduce new profile fields frequently. Which type of NoSQL database would be most suitable for this use case, and why?
    *   **Correct Answer & Explanation:** A **Document Database** (like MongoDB) would be most suitable.
        *   **Why:** Document databases are schema-less or have flexible schemas, meaning each user profile (document) can have a different structure without requiring a predefined table schema. This perfectly accommodates the varying fields in user profiles and allows for rapid feature development by simply adding new fields to documents as needed, without complex database migrations. Relational databases would struggle with this flexibility, requiring frequent schema alterations or complex EAV (Entity-Attribute-Value) models.

2.  **Question:** Explain the CAP theorem in the context of NoSQL databases. If a distributed NoSQL system prioritizes Availability and Partition Tolerance, what is the trade-off, and what does this imply for data consistency?
    *   **Correct Answer & Explanation:** The **CAP theorem** states that a distributed system can only guarantee two out of three properties: **Consistency**, **Availability**, and **Partition Tolerance**.
        *   **Consistency:** All clients see the same data at the same time, regardless of which node they connect to.
        *   **Availability:** Every request receives a response (not an error), without guarantee that it contains the most recent version of the information.
        *   **Partition Tolerance:** The system continues to operate despite network partitions (communication failures between nodes).
        If a distributed NoSQL system prioritizes **Availability** and **Partition Tolerance**, the trade-off is **Consistency**. This implies that the system will likely exhibit **eventual consistency**. In an eventually consistent system, updates might not be immediately visible across all nodes. If a network partition occurs, different nodes might temporarily hold different versions of the same data. The system will remain available and functional, but it will take some time for all data replicas to synchronize and for all clients to eventually see the same, consistent state. This is acceptable for many applications where immediate consistency is not critical (e.g., social media feeds, IoT sensor data), but it's a critical consideration for applications requiring strong transactional consistency (e.g., banking systems).

#### AI generation note
Create a 14-minute mixed-format lesson. Start with an animated infographic explaining the motivations for NoSQL and the four main types (document, key-value, column-family, graph) with simple visual metaphors for each. Transition to a live coding session using the MongoDB Shell (or PyMongo in a Jupyter Notebook) to demonstrate inserting, finding, updating, and deleting documents in a `sensor_data` collection. Show how to query nested fields and array elements. Include a visual overlay explaining the CAP theorem with a clear diagram showing the trade-offs. End with a reflection prompt asking learners to consider a real-world scenario (e.g., storing user preferences for a streaming service) and decide if SQL or NoSQL is better, justifying their choice.

### Chapter 3.4 — Data Collection Techniques and APIs

#### Learning objectives
*   Understand the principles and ethical considerations involved in web scraping.
*   Implement basic web scraping techniques using Python libraries to extract data from web pages.
*   Explain how to interact with RESTful APIs to programmatically retrieve data.
*   Construct Python code to make API requests, handle responses, and parse JSON data.
*   Identify various data ingestion strategies beyond web scraping and APIs, including cloud storage and streaming data.
*   Recognize common pitfalls and best practices in data collection, including error handling and rate limiting.

#### Detailed lesson content
Once you understand where data lives and its various forms, the next crucial step is learning how to acquire it. Data collection is the process of gathering information from various sources to prepare it for analysis. While internal data might be readily available in databases, external data often requires more active collection methods. Two of the most common and powerful techniques for external data acquisition are web scraping and utilizing Application Programming Interfaces (APIs).

**Web scraping** involves programmatically extracting data from websites. It's like having a robot browse web pages and copy specific information for you. This technique is invaluable for gathering public data that isn't offered through a structured API, such as product prices from e-commerce sites, news articles, or public directories. However, web scraping comes with significant **ethical and legal considerations**.

**Ethical and Safety Notes for Web Scraping:**
1.  **Check `robots.txt`:** Most websites have a `robots.txt` file (e.g., `www.example.com/robots.txt`) that specifies which parts of the site crawlers are allowed or disallowed from accessing. Always respect these directives.
2.  **Review Terms of Service:** Many websites explicitly forbid scraping in their terms of service. Violating these terms can lead to legal action or IP blocking.
3.  **Rate Limiting:** Do not bombard a website with requests. Send requests slowly and add delays between them to avoid overloading their servers. Excessive requests can be seen as a Denial-of-Service (DoS) attack.
4.  **Identify Yourself:** Include a user-agent string in your requests that identifies your scraper (e.g., `MyDataScienceBot/1.0`). This allows website administrators to contact you if there are issues.
5.  **Scrape Responsibly:** Only collect data that is publicly available and necessary for your analysis. Avoid scraping personal or sensitive information.

For practical web scraping in Python, the `requests` library is used to make HTTP requests to fetch web page content, and `BeautifulSoup` (from `bs4`) is used to parse the HTML and navigate its structure.

```python
import requests
from bs4 import BeautifulSoup
import time # For adding delays

url = "http://quotes.toscrape.com/" # A site specifically designed for scraping practice

try:
    response = requests.get(url)
    response.raise_for_status() # Raise an exception for HTTP errors (4xx or 5xx)

    soup = BeautifulSoup(response.text, 'html.parser')

    # Find all quote divs
    quotes = soup.find_all('div', class_='quote')

    print(f"--- Scraped Quotes from {url} ---")
    for quote in quotes:
        text = quote.find('span', class_='text').text
        author = quote.find('small', class_='author').text
        tags = [tag.text for tag in quote.find('div', class_='tags').find_all('a', class_='tag')]
        print(f"Quote: {text}")
        print(f"Author: {author}")
        print(f"Tags: {', '.join(tags)}")
        print("-" * 30)

    # Common mistake: Not handling potential missing elements
    # If a 'span' with class 'text' wasn't found, .find() would return None, and .text would raise an error.
    # Always check if the element exists:
    # text_element = quote.find('span', class_='text')
    # if text_element:
    #     text = text_element.text
    # else:
    #     text = "N/A"

except requests.exceptions.RequestException as e:
    print(f"Error fetching URL: {e}")
except Exception as e:
    print(f"An unexpected error occurred: {e}")

time.sleep(1) # Be polite, add a delay
```

While web scraping is powerful, the preferred method for data acquisition is often through **APIs (Application Programming Interfaces)**. An API is a set of rules and protocols that allows different software applications to communicate with each other. Many web services, social media platforms, and data providers offer APIs to programmatically access their data in a structured and controlled manner. This is generally more reliable, faster, and less prone to breaking than web scraping, as the data format is explicitly defined.

Most modern APIs are **RESTful APIs**, which use standard HTTP methods (GET, POST, PUT, DELETE) to interact with resources. For data scientists, the `GET` method is most common for retrieving data. API responses are typically formatted in JSON or XML.

To use an API, you'll need:
1.  **API Endpoint:** The specific URL that provides access to the resource.
2.  **Authentication (Optional but common):** Many APIs require an API key, token, or OAuth to verify your identity and authorize access.
3.  **Parameters:** Query parameters or request body data to filter or specify the data you want.
4.  **Error Handling:** APIs will return HTTP status codes (e.g., 200 OK, 404 Not Found, 401 Unauthorized, 429 Too Many Requests) and error messages.

Let's use a public API example, `JSONPlaceholder`, which provides fake online REST API for testing and prototyping.

```python
import requests
import json

# API Endpoint for posts
api_url = "https://jsonplaceholder.typicode.com/posts"

try:
    # Make a GET request to the API
    response = requests.get(api_url)
    response.raise_for_status() # Raise an exception for HTTP errors

    # Parse the JSON response
    posts = response.json()

    print(f"--- Data from JSONPlaceholder API ({api_url}) ---")
    # Print the first 3 posts as an example
    for i, post in enumerate(posts[:3]):
        print(f"Post ID: {post['id']}")
        print(f"Title: {post['title']}")
        print(f"Body: {post['body'][:70]}...") # Truncate body for display
        print("-" * 30)

    # Example: Fetch a specific post by ID
    single_post_url = f"{api_url}/1"
    single_post_response = requests.get(single_post_url)
    single_post_response.raise_for_status()
    single_post = single_post_response.json()
    print(f"\n--- Single Post (ID 1) ---")
    print(f"Title: {single_post['title']}")
    print(f"Body: {single_post['body']}")

except requests.exceptions.RequestException as e:
    print(f"API Request Error: {e}")
except json.JSONDecodeError as e:
    print(f"Error decoding JSON response: {e}")
except Exception as e:
    print(f"An unexpected error occurred: {e}")

# Common mistake: Not handling API rate limits.
# If an API has rate limits (e.g., 100 requests per minute), you need to implement delays or retry logic.
# Some APIs include 'Retry-After' headers in 429 responses to guide you.
```

Beyond web scraping and APIs, data scientists employ various other **data ingestion strategies**:
*   **Database Exports:** Extracting data directly from relational (SQL) or non-relational (NoSQL) databases. This is common for internal data.
*   **File Ingestion:** Reading data from local files (CSV, JSON, Excel, Parquet, Avro) stored on local machines, network drives, or cloud storage services like **IBM Cloud Object Storage** or AWS S3. Python libraries like `pandas` are excellent for this.
*   **Streaming Data:** For real-time analytics, data can be ingested from continuous streams generated by IoT devices, social media feeds, financial tickers, or application logs. Technologies like Apache Kafka, Apache Flink, or cloud-native streaming services (e.g., IBM Event Streams) are used here.
*   **Data Warehouses/Lakes:** Large organizations often centralize their data in data warehouses (structured, optimized for reporting) or data lakes (raw, diverse data for advanced analytics). Data scientists typically query these directly.
*   **ETL/ELT Pipelines:** Extract, Transform, Load (ETL) or Extract, Load, Transform (ELT) processes are used to move and prepare data from various sources into a target system (like a data warehouse or data lake).

**Common Mistakes in Data Collection:**
*   **Ignoring Error Handling:** Network issues, API limits, malformed HTML, or unexpected data formats can cause scripts to crash. Always implement `try-except` blocks.
*   **Not Validating Data:** Data collected from external sources is often messy. Always validate data types, check for missing values, and look for outliers immediately after collection.
*   **Overlooking Data Volume:** Be mindful of the amount of data you're collecting. Large datasets require efficient storage and processing strategies.
*   **Security Vulnerabilities:** Hardcoding API keys directly in scripts is a security risk. Use environment variables or secure configuration management.
*   **Lack of Documentation:** Document your data sources, collection methods, and any transformations applied. This ensures reproducibility and understanding for future analysis.

Effective data collection is a blend of technical skill, ethical awareness, and meticulous planning. It's the first step in transforming raw information into actionable insights.

#### Key concepts
*   **Web Scraping**: Programmatically extracting data from websites by parsing their HTML content.
*   **`robots.txt`**: A file on a website that tells web robots (like scrapers) which areas of the site they should not crawl.
*   **Rate Limiting**: Restricting the number of requests a client can make to a server within a given time period to prevent abuse and server overload.
*   **API (Application Programming Interface)**: A set of rules and protocols that allows different software applications to communicate and exchange data.
*   **RESTful API**: An architectural style for designing networked applications, using standard HTTP methods for communication and typically returning data in JSON or XML.
*   **API Endpoint**: A specific URL that represents a resource or a function that can be accessed via an API.
*   **HTTP Methods**: Standard actions performed on web resources (e.g., GET for retrieving, POST for creating, PUT for updating, DELETE for removing).
*   **`requests` (Python Library)**: A popular Python library for making HTTP requests to web servers and APIs.
*   **`BeautifulSoup` (Python Library)**: A Python library for parsing HTML and XML documents, making it easy to extract data.
*   **JSON (JavaScript Object Notation)**: A common, lightweight data-interchange format used by APIs.
*   **Data Ingestion**: The process of importing, transferring, and processing data for use or storage in a database or data lake.
*   **Streaming Data**: Data that is generated continuously and processed in real-time or near real-time.
*   **IBM Cloud Object Storage**: A highly scalable and cost-effective cloud storage service for unstructured data, often used for data lakes.

#### Hands-on activity
**Activity: Collecting Data from a Public API and Web Page**

You will write a Python script to:
1.  Fetch data from a public API (`JSONPlaceholder`).
2.  Perform a basic web scrape on a practice site (`quotes.toscrape.com`).
3.  Combine and print a summary of the collected data.

**Code Template:**

```python
import requests
from bs4 import BeautifulSoup
import json
import time

# --- Part 1: Collect data from a Public API (JSONPlaceholder) ---
def fetch_api_data(url):
    """Fetches data from a given API URL and returns parsed JSON."""
    try:
        print(f"\n--- Fetching API data from: {url} ---")
        response = requests.get(url)
        response.raise_for_status() # Raise an exception for HTTP errors
        data = response.json()
        print(f"Successfully fetched {len(data)} items from API.")
        return data
    except requests.exceptions.RequestException as e:
        print(f"API Request Error: {e}")
        return None
    except json.JSONDecodeError as e:
        print(f"Error decoding JSON response from API: {e}")
        return None

# --- Part 2: Scrape data from a web page (Quotes to Scrape) ---
def scrape_web_data(url, delay_seconds=1):
    """Scrapes quotes from a given URL and returns a list of dictionaries."""
    quotes_list = []
    try:
        print(f"\n--- Scraping web data from: {url} ---")
        response = requests.get(url)
        response.raise_for_status()
        soup = BeautifulSoup(response.text, 'html.parser')

        quotes = soup.find_all('div', class_='quote')
        for quote in quotes:
            text_element = quote.find('span', class_='text')
            author_element = quote.find('small', class_='author')
            tags_div = quote.find('div', class_='tags')

            text = text_element.text if text_element else "N/A"
            author = author_element.text if author_element else "Unknown"
            tags = [tag.text for tag in tags_div.find_all('a', class_='tag')] if tags_div else []

            quotes_list.append({
                'quote_text': text,
                'author': author,
                'tags': tags
            })
        print(f"Successfully scraped {len(quotes_list)} quotes.")
    except requests.exceptions.RequestException as e:
        print(f"Web Scraping Request Error: {e}")
    except Exception as e:
        print(f"An unexpected error occurred during scraping: {e}")
    finally:
        time.sleep(delay_seconds) # Be polite
    return quotes_list

# --- Main execution ---
if __name__ == "__main__":
    # API data collection
    posts_api_url = "https://jsonplaceholder.typicode.com/posts"
    posts_data = fetch_api_data(posts_api_url)

    if posts_data:
        print("\n--- Summary of API Posts ---")
        for i, post in enumerate(posts_data[:5]): # Print first 5 posts
            print(f"  Post {i+1}: ID={post['id']}, Title='{post['title'][:50]}...'")

    # Web scraping data collection
    quotes_web_url = "http://quotes.toscrape.com/"
    quotes_data = scrape_web_data(quotes_web_url, delay_seconds=2) # Longer delay for scraping

    if quotes_data:
        print("\n--- Summary of Scraped Quotes ---")
        for i, quote in enumerate(quotes_data[:5]): # Print first 5 quotes
            print(f"  Quote {i+1}: '{quote['quote_text'][:50]}...' by {quote['author']}")

    print("\nData collection complete. Review the printed summaries.")
    # Challenge: You could further process this data, e.g., save to CSV or analyze.
```

**Instructions:**
1.  Save the code as `data_collector.py`.
2.  Ensure you have `requests` and `beautifulsoup4` installed: `pip install requests beautifulsoup4`.
3.  Run the script from your terminal: `python data_collector.py`.
4.  Observe the output, which will show the data fetched from the API and scraped from the website.
5.  **Challenge:** Modify the web scraping part to extract quotes from the *next page* of `quotes.toscrape.com` (you'll need to find the "next" button's link and loop). Remember to include `time.sleep()` between requests.

#### Assessment idea
1.  **Question:** You are tasked with collecting real-time stock prices for a financial analytics project. You discover that a major stock exchange provides a public API with a dedicated endpoint for fetching current prices. However, a colleague suggests simply scraping the prices from the exchange's public website. Discuss the advantages of using the API over web scraping for this specific task, considering reliability, efficiency, and ethical implications.
    *   **Correct Answer & Explanation:** Using the API is significantly advantageous for collecting real-time stock prices:
        *   **Reliability:** APIs provide structured, consistent data formats (e.g., JSON), which are less likely to change compared to a website's HTML structure. Web scraping is fragile; even minor website layout changes can break your scraper.
        *   **Efficiency:** APIs are designed for programmatic access, offering faster data retrieval with less overhead than parsing entire HTML pages. They often allow for direct querying of specific data points.
        *   **Ethical/Legal:** Using an API is the intended way for programmatic access, respecting the data provider's terms of service. Web scraping, especially at high frequencies for real-time data, can violate `robots.txt` rules, terms of service, and potentially overload servers, leading to IP blocking or legal issues.
        *   **Rate Limits:** While APIs have rate limits, they are typically well-defined and manageable, allowing for predictable data flow, whereas scraping might face unpredictable blocking.

2.  **Question:** A data scientist is building a system to monitor social media mentions of a company's brand. They plan to use the `requests` and `BeautifulSoup` libraries in Python to scrape popular social media platforms. What are two critical ethical or practical considerations they must address before deploying such a scraper, and how would they mitigate these?
    *   **Correct Answer & Explanation:**
        *   **Consideration 1: Terms of Service and `robots.txt`:** Social media platforms almost universally prohibit scraping in their terms of service, and their `robots.txt` files often disallow crawling. **Mitigation:** The data scientist must thoroughly review the terms of service and `robots.txt` for each platform. If scraping is prohibited, they should *not* proceed. Instead, they should explore official APIs provided by the social media platforms (e.g., Twitter API, Facebook Graph API) which are the sanctioned and ethical way to access data.
        *   **Consideration 2: Rate Limiting and Server Load:** Aggressive scraping can put a heavy load on the social media platform's servers, potentially causing performance issues for legitimate users or leading to the scraper's IP being blocked. **Mitigation:** Implement significant delays (`time.sleep()`) between requests to mimic human browsing behavior and reduce server strain. Start with very conservative delays and only increase frequency if absolutely necessary and permitted. Additionally, use a polite `User-Agent` header to identify the scraper. If an API is available, it typically handles rate limiting more gracefully and provides clearer guidelines.

---

## Module 4: Data Exploration and Visualization

**Module Goal:** To equip learners with the fundamental skills to explore, clean, and visualize data effectively using Python libraries, enabling them to uncover insights and communicate findings clearly.

### Chapter 4.1 — Introduction to Data Exploration and Descriptive Statistics

#### Learning objectives
*   Identify different types of data (numerical, categorical, ordinal) and their characteristics.
*   Perform initial data inspection using Pandas to understand dataset structure and content.
*   Calculate and interpret key descriptive statistics such as mean, median, mode, variance, and standard deviation.
*   Utilize Pandas functions to summarize data and identify potential issues like missing values or incorrect data types.
*   Explain the importance of data exploration as a foundational step in the data science workflow.

#### Detailed lesson content
Welcome to the exciting world of data exploration and visualization! This module is where we truly begin to uncover the stories hidden within our data. Before we can build sophisticated models or make predictions, we need to understand our data intimately. Think of data exploration as getting to know a new friend – you wouldn't just assume things about them; you'd ask questions, observe their behavior, and learn their characteristics. In data science, this involves systematically examining the dataset to discover patterns, detect anomalies, test hypotheses, and check assumptions with the help of descriptive statistics and graphical representations.

Our journey begins with understanding the fundamental types of data we'll encounter: numerical and categorical. Numerical data, as the name suggests, represents quantities and can be either discrete (countable integers, like the number of cars) or continuous (measurable values that can take any value within a range, like temperature or height). Categorical data, on the other hand, represents qualities or characteristics, often non-numeric. This can be nominal (categories without inherent order, like colors or gender) or ordinal (categories with a meaningful order, like 'small', 'medium', 'large'). Recognizing these distinctions is crucial because the type of data dictates which statistical methods and visualizations are appropriate. For instance, calculating the mean of nominal categorical data makes no sense, but it's a fundamental operation for numerical data.

With our understanding of data types in hand, we turn to the powerful Python library, Pandas, which is indispensable for data manipulation and analysis. When you first load a dataset, your immediate goal is to get a high-level overview. The `df.head()` method is your first stop, displaying the first few rows of your DataFrame, giving you a quick peek at the data's structure and content. Following this, `df.info()` provides a concise summary, including the number of entries, the number of columns, non-null values per column, and the data type (Dtype) of each column. This is incredibly useful for spotting columns with many missing values or columns that have been incorrectly interpreted (e.g., numbers stored as strings). A common mistake beginners make is to overlook `df.info()` and jump straight into calculations, only to be met with errors due to mismatched data types. Always check your Dtypes!

Beyond structural inspection, descriptive statistics offer a quantitative summary of your data. The `df.describe()` method is a workhorse here, providing statistics like count, mean, standard deviation, minimum, 25th percentile, median (50th percentile), 75th percentile, and maximum for numerical columns. For categorical columns, you might use `df.describe(include='object')` or `df.value_counts()` to see the frequency of each unique category. The mean (average) tells us the central tendency, but it's sensitive to outliers. The median, the middle value when data is ordered, is more robust to extreme values. The mode, the most frequent value, is useful for both numerical and categorical data. Variance and standard deviation measure the spread or dispersion of the data points around the mean. A high standard deviation indicates that data points are spread out over a wider range of values, while a low standard deviation indicates that data points are clustered closer to the mean. Understanding these measures helps us grasp the distribution and variability within our dataset. For example, if a dataset of customer ages has a mean of 35 and a standard deviation of 10, it tells us that most customers are around 35 years old, with a typical spread of 10 years above or below that.

Let's consider a practical scenario. Imagine you're analyzing a dataset of customer transactions. You load it into a Pandas DataFrame and immediately use `df.head()` to see columns like 'CustomerID', 'Product', 'Price', 'Quantity', and 'TransactionDate'. Then `df.info()` reveals that 'Price' and 'Quantity' are correctly identified as numerical, but 'TransactionDate' is an 'object' (string) type, which will need conversion later for time-series analysis. You might also notice that the 'Product' column has fewer non-null values than other columns, indicating missing product information. Next, `df.describe()` on 'Price' and 'Quantity' shows the average price, the range of quantities, and helps you identify any unusually high or low values that might be outliers. For 'Product', `df['Product'].value_counts()` would show the most frequently purchased items, giving you immediate insights into popular products. This systematic initial exploration helps you form hypotheses, identify data quality issues, and plan your next steps for cleaning and further analysis. Remember, the goal isn't just to run commands, but to interpret their output and understand what the data is telling you. This iterative process of questioning, inspecting, and summarizing is the bedrock of effective data science.

```python
import pandas as pd
import numpy as np

# Sample Data: Customer Transaction Data
data = {
    'TransactionID': range(1, 11),
    'CustomerID': [101, 102, 103, 101, 104, 105, 102, 106, 103, 107],
    'ProductCategory': ['Electronics', 'Books', 'Clothing', 'Electronics', 'Books', 'Electronics', 'Clothing', 'Books', 'Electronics', 'Clothing'],
    'Price': [1200.50, 25.99, 50.00, 1500.00, 30.50, 800.00, 65.00, 15.00, 1300.00, 45.00],
    'Quantity': [1, 2, 1, 1, 3, 1, 2, 1, 1, 1],
    'TransactionDate': pd.to_datetime(['2023-01-01', '2023-01-02', '2023-01-03', '2023-01-04', '2023-01-05', '2023-01-06', '2023-01-07', '2023-01-08', '2023-01-09', '2023-01-10']),
    'PaymentMethod': ['Credit Card', 'Debit Card', 'Credit Card', 'PayPal', 'Credit Card', 'Debit Card', 'Credit Card', 'PayPal', 'Credit Card', 'Debit Card']
}
df = pd.DataFrame(data)

# Add some missing values for demonstration
df.loc[2, 'Price'] = np.nan
df.loc[5, 'ProductCategory'] = np.nan
df.loc[8, 'Quantity'] = np.nan

print("--- Initial Data Inspection ---")
print("df.head():")
print(df.head())

print("\n--- DataFrame Information ---")
print("df.info():")
df.info()

print("\n--- Descriptive Statistics for Numerical Columns ---")
print("df.describe():")
print(df.describe())

print("\n--- Value Counts for Categorical Column 'ProductCategory' ---")
print("df['ProductCategory'].value_counts():")
print(df['ProductCategory'].value_counts())

print("\n--- Value Counts for Categorical Column 'PaymentMethod' ---")
print("df['PaymentMethod'].value_counts():")
print(df['PaymentMethod'].value_counts())

print("\n--- Checking for Missing Values ---")
print("df.isnull().sum():")
print(df.isnull().sum())
```

#### Key concepts
*   **Data Exploration:** The initial process of analyzing data to summarize its main characteristics, often using visual methods.
*   **Descriptive Statistics:** Quantitative summaries of data, including measures of central tendency (mean, median, mode) and dispersion (variance, standard deviation).
*   **Numerical Data:** Data representing quantities, which can be discrete (countable) or continuous (measurable).
*   **Categorical Data:** Data representing qualities or characteristics, which can be nominal (unordered categories) or ordinal (ordered categories).
*   **Pandas DataFrame:** A 2-dimensional labeled data structure with columns of potentially different types, commonly used for data manipulation in Python.
*   **Mean:** The average of a set of numerical values.
*   **Median:** The middle value in an ordered set of numerical values.
*   **Mode:** The most frequently occurring value in a dataset.
*   **Variance:** A measure of how spread out a set of data is from its mean.
*   **Standard Deviation:** The square root of the variance, indicating the typical distance of data points from the mean.

#### Hands-on activity

#### Assessment idea
1.  **Question:** You are given a dataset of customer reviews for a product. One column, 'Rating', contains values from 1 to 5. Another column, 'ReviewText', contains the actual text of the review. Which of the following descriptive statistics would be most appropriate for the 'Rating' column, and why?
    a) Mean and Standard Deviation
    b) Mode and Value Counts
    c) Median and Interquartile Range
    d) All of the above are equally appropriate.

    **Correct Answer:** a) Mean and Standard Deviation.
    **Explanation:** While 'Rating' values (1-5) might appear categorical, they represent an ordered scale where the difference between 1 and 2 is meaningfully similar to the difference between 4 and 5. This makes it ordinal-numerical data. Therefore, calculating the mean and standard deviation provides a good measure of central tendency and spread, indicating the average rating and how much ratings typically vary from that average. Mode and value counts are also useful, but mean and standard deviation offer a more complete picture for this type of ordered numerical scale.

2.  **Question:** After loading a new dataset into a Pandas DataFrame `df`, you run `df.info()` and notice that the 'PurchaseDate' column is listed as `object` Dtype, even though it contains dates like '2023-10-26'. What is a common implication of this, and what is the immediate next step you should consider for this column if you plan to analyze trends over time?

    **Correct Answer:**
    **Implication:** When `df.info()` shows a date column as `object` Dtype, it means Pandas has interpreted the dates as generic strings. This prevents you from performing date-specific operations like extracting the year/month, calculating time differences, or filtering by date ranges directly.
    **Immediate Next Step:** The immediate next step should be to convert the 'PurchaseDate' column to a proper datetime Dtype using `pd.to_datetime()`. For example: `df['PurchaseDate'] = pd.to_datetime(df['PurchaseDate'])`. This conversion allows Pandas to recognize the column as containing date and time information, enabling powerful time-series analysis capabilities.
